Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 19779 ms + 965 ms + 10659 ms = 31403 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 6059 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 37463 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 31403 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 31403 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 51506 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 5798 ms, comparable BL 3915 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 59256 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 26431 ms + 1103 ms + 17429 ms = 44963 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 9884 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 54847 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 44963 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 44963 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 76748 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 8717 ms, comparable BL 5387 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 85600 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

union of overlapping request waits		 8017 ms 3915 ms
same TP-served rows

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 71867 ms + 1137 ms + 41342 ms = 114345 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 5228 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 119573 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 114345 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 114345 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 51506 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 8017 ms, comparable BL 3915 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 59256 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 36611 ms + 1318 ms + 17406 ms = 55334 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 8088 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 63422 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 55334 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 55334 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 76748 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 8307 ms, comparable BL 5387 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 85600 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 33312 ms + 1015 ms + 66044 ms = 100371 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 4960 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 105331 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 100371 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 100371 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 51285 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 10591 ms, comparable BL 3726 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 59256 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 28758 ms + 1107 ms + 12684 ms = 42549 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 6047 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 48596 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 42549 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 42549 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 76752 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 6619 ms, comparable BL 5391 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 85600 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 22124 ms + 0 ms + 24137 ms = 46261 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 21930 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 68192 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 46261 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 46261 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 51506 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 7377 ms, comparable BL 3915 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 59256 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Reading the totals

TP asset work sum = sum of unique pack/file fetch work + per-asset extract + per-asset overhead across served-from-pack assets = 55677 ms + 0 ms + 43076 ms = 98753 ms
Excluded duplicated waits: 19672 ms. Raw paused sum before excluding waits: 118425 ms.

TP effective asset wait = TP asset wait sum + sum of baseline-fetch ms for assets TP fell through on
= 98753 ms + 0 ms (0 fall-throughs paid network cost) = 98753 ms

Comparable BL asset wait sum = baseline fetch sum for rows TP served = 76752 ms
Request window collapses overlapping waits into a union: TP 7344 ms, comparable BL 5391 ms.
All observed BL asset wait sum = 85600 ms

These are overlapping column sums, not page-load wall-clock. They can be lower or higher than the top-level duration delta because asset requests run concurrently and because top-level load includes parse/eval/layout/app work outside these asset rows. Use iteration durations as the real wall-clock answer; use these sums only to understand where asset-level time is spent.

Reading this table

  • Pack/file fetch+decode — full/stream variants show the time this request waited for pack initialization. Range-stream variants show metadata wait plus file-region fetch wait. miss is the first initializer for a pack, pending means this asset arrived while another request was already initializing the same pack, and hit means metadata/decode was already available at request start.
  • Baseline asset fetch — time to fetch this single asset over the wire in baseline mode. Adjacent for direct comparison.
  • Extract — pack.extract(file), in-memory, sub-millisecond.
  • Harness IPC tax — total − fetch − extract. Pure overhead from this measurement rig: Playwright routing, page.evaluate CDP roundtrip, browser-to-Node string transfer, route.fulfill, and queueing when many requests fire concurrently. Zero in a real Service-Worker deployment.
  • TokenPack total — fetch + extract + overhead. The wall-clock time spent producing this asset response.
  • Green/red coloring compares real work (fetch + extract) vs baseline fetch — i.e., what TokenPack would cost without the harness tax. Green on the TokenPack side = real work was faster than the baseline fetch.
  • outside TP manifest — baseline fetched this URL, but it is outside the comparable manifest-handled URL set for this sampled phase. This is not a TokenPack miss.
  • TP did not request — baseline fetched this comparable manifest URL, but the TokenPack run did not request it in the sampled phase. A large cluster usually means the TP page diverged or failed before reaching that import path; an isolated row can also come from feature flags, lazy imports, version drift, or conditional app flow.
  • TP-only (not in BL) — TokenPack requested this URL but baseline never did. Different code path got executed (feature flag, A/B variant, error-recovery branch).

Column sums overstate wall-clock because requests run in parallel. Iteration durations are the real answer.

Legend
  • Baseline — page run with recorded static assets fulfilled from the local isolated asset store. Live document/API requests may still go to origin.
  • Baseline-CDP — same CDP fulfill rig as TP for manifest assets, but bytes come from the recorded local asset store instead of live origin.
  • TokenPack — intercept + serve from packed PNG. Pays harness IPC tax on every served asset.
  • Δ TP vs BL (prod fairness) — TP minus baseline. Real comparison including harness handicap. If TP wins here, it wins despite the rig.
  • Δ TP vs BL-CDP (tech fairness) — TP minus baseline-CDP. Both pay harness tax, so this isolates pack-vs-direct. Tech-level win.
  • Est. prod TP (–harness) — projection of TP duration with harness IPC tax stripped out. Computed as TP × (1 − harnessRatio) where harnessRatio = sum(harness)/sum(totalMs) across all served-from-pack assets. Approximates a real Service-Worker deployment where the browser delivers pack bytes directly without CDP roundtrips. Estimate, not measurement.
